```java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.function.Consumer;
import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.Objects;

public class React {

    public static class Cell<T> {
        T value;
        List<Cell<T>> dependents = new ArrayList<>();
        boolean changed = false;

        public T getValue() {
            return value;
        }
    }

    public static class InputCell<T> extends Cell<T> {
        public void setValue(T newValue) {
            if (Objects.equals(newValue, this.value)) {
                return;
            }
            this.value = newValue;
            this.changed = true;
            propagate();
        }

        private void propagate() {
            List<Cell<T>> toUpdate = new ArrayList<>();
            Set<Cell<T>> visited = new HashSet<>();
            
            dfs(this, visited, toUpdate);
            
            // toUpdate is in post-order (dependents before dependencies)
            // Reverse to get topological order (dependencies before dependents)
            Collections.reverse(toUpdate);
            
            // Remove the input cell itself
            toUpdate.remove(this);
            
            // Store old values before updating
            Map<Cell<T>, T> oldValues = new HashMap<>();
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    oldValues.put(c, c.value);
                }
            }
            
            // Update all cells in topological order
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    ((ComputeCell<T>) c).recompute();
                }
            }
            
            // Trigger callbacks only for cells whose values actually changed
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    ComputeCell<T> computeCell = (ComputeCell<T>) c;
                    T oldValue = oldValues.get(c);
                    if (!Objects.equals(oldValue, computeCell.value)) {
                        computeCell.triggerCallbacks();
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        private void dfs(Cell<T> cell, Set<Cell<T>> visited, List<Cell<T>> toUpdate) {
            if (visited.contains(cell)) {
                return;
            }
            visited.add(cell);
            for (Cell<T> dep : cell.dependents) {
                dfs(dep, visited, toUpdate);
            }
            toUpdate.add(cell);
        }
    }

    public static class ComputeCell<T> extends Cell<T> {
        Function<List<T>, T> function;
        List<Cell<T>> dependencies;
        List<Consumer<T>> callbacks = new ArrayList<>();

        public ComputeCell(Function<List<T>, T> function, List<Cell<T>> dependencies) {
            this.function = function;
            this.dependencies = dependencies;
            for (Cell<T> dep : dependencies) {
                dep.dependents.add(this);
            }
            this.value = function.apply(getDependencyValues());
        }

        private List<T> getDependencyValues() {
            List<T> values = new ArrayList<>();
            for (Cell<T> dep : dependencies) {
                values.add(dep.value);
            }
            return values;
        }

        void recompute() {
            T newVal = function.apply(getDependencyValues());
            if (!Objects.equals(newVal, this.value)) {
                this.value = newVal;
                this.changed = true;
            } else {
                this.changed = false;
            }
        }

        void triggerCallbacks() {
            for (Consumer<T> callback : callbacks) {
                callback.accept(this.value);
            }
        }

        public void addCallback(Consumer<T> callback) {
            callbacks.add(callback);
        }

        public void removeCallback(Consumer<T> callback) {
            callbacks.remove(callback);
        }
    }

    public static <T> InputCell<T> inputCell(T initialValue) {
        InputCell<T> cell = new InputCell<>();
        cell.value = initialValue;
        return cell;
    }

    public static <T> ComputeCell<T> computeCell(Function<List<T>, T> function, List<Cell<T>> cells) {
        return new ComputeCell<>(function, cells);
    }
}
```

One minor thing: `dfs` adds `cell` to `toUpdate` after visiting dependents. So `toUpdate` will have `InputCell` at the end. After reverse, `InputCell` is at the beginning. `toUpdate.remove(this)` removes it. Correct.

`input1` -> `compute1` -> `compute2` -> `compute3`
`input1` -> `compute3`
DFS:
`input1` -> visit `compute1`
  `compute1` -> visit `compute2`
    `compute2` -> visit `compute3`
      `compute3` -> no dependents. add `compute3`.
    add `compute2`.
  add `compute1`.
`input1` -> visit `compute3` (visited).
add `input1`.
`toUpdate`: `[compute3, compute2, compute1, input1]`
Reverse: `[input1, compute1, compute2, compute3]`
Remove `input1`: `[compute1, compute2, compute3]`
Order is correct.

What about diamond case?
`input1` -> `compute1` -> `compute3`
`input1` -> `compute2` -> `compute3`
DFS:
`input1` -> visit `compute1`
  `compute1` -> visit `compute3`
    `compute3` -> no dependents. add `compute3`.
  add `compute1`.
`input1` -> visit `compute2`
  `compute2` -> visit `compute3` (visited).
  add `compute2`.
add `input1`.
`toUpdate`: `[compute3, compute1, compute2, input1]`
Reverse: `[input1, compute2, compute1, compute3]`
Remove `input1`: `[compute2, compute1, compute3]`
Order: `compute2`, `compute1`, `compute3`.
Both `compute1` and `compute2` are before `compute3`. Correct.
The relative order of `compute1` and `compute2` doesn't matter since they don't depend on each other.

import java.util.*;
import java.util.function.Consumer;
import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.Objects;

public class React {

    public static class Cell<T> {
        T value;
        List<Cell<T>> dependents = new ArrayList<>();
        boolean changed = false;

        public T getValue() {
            return value;
        }
    }

    public static class InputCell<T> extends Cell<T> {
        public void setValue(T newValue) {

                return;
            }
            this.value = newValue;
            this.changed = true;
            propagate();
        }

        private void propagate() {

            Set<Cell<T>> visited = new HashSet<>();
            
            dfs(this, visited, toUpdate);
            
            Collections.reverse(toUpdate);
            toUpdate.remove(this);
            
            Map<Cell<T>, T> oldValues = new HashMap<>();
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    oldValues.put(c, c.value);
                }

                    T oldValue = oldValues.get(c);
                    if (!Objects.equals(oldValue, computeCell.value)) {
                        computeCell.triggerCallbacks();

            if (visited.contains(cell)) {
                return;
            }
            visited.add(cell);
            for (Cell<T> dep : cell.dependents) {
                dfs(dep, visited, toUpdate);
            }
            toUpdate.add(cell);

        Function<List<T>, T> function;
        List<Cell<T>> dependencies;

```java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.function.Consumer;
import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.Objects;

public class React {

    public static class Cell<T> {
        T value;
        List<Cell<T>> dependents = new ArrayList<>();
        boolean changed = false;

        public T getValue() {
            return value;
        }
    }

    public static class InputCell<T> extends Cell<T> {
        public void setValue(T newValue) {
            if (Objects.equals(newValue, this.value)) {
                return;
            }
            this.value = newValue;
            this.changed = true;
            propagate();
        }

        private void propagate() {
            List<Cell<T>> toUpdate = new ArrayList<>();
            Set<Cell<T>> visited = new HashSet<>();
            
            dfs(this, visited, toUpdate);
            
            // toUpdate is in post-order (dependents before dependencies)
            // Reverse to get topological order (dependencies before dependents)
            Collections.reverse(toUpdate);
            
            // Remove the input cell itself from the update list
            toUpdate.remove(this);
            
            // Store old values before updating
            Map<Cell<T>, T> oldValues = new HashMap<>();
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    oldValues.put(c, c.value);
                }
            }
            
            // Update all cells in topological order
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    ((ComputeCell<T>) c).recompute();
                }
            }
            
            // Trigger callbacks only for cells whose values actually changed
            for (Cell<T> c : toUpdate) {
                if (c instanceof ComputeCell) {
                    ComputeCell<T> computeCell = (ComputeCell<T>) c;
                    T oldValue = oldValues.get(c);
                    if (!Objects.equals(oldValue, computeCell.value)) {
                        computeCell.triggerCallbacks();
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        private void dfs(Cell<T> cell, Set<Cell<T>> visited, List<Cell<T>> toUpdate) {
            if (visited.contains(cell)) {
                return;
            }
            visited.add(cell);
            for (Cell<T> dep : cell.dependents) {
                dfs(dep, visited, toUpdate);
            }
            toUpdate.add(cell);
        }
    }

    public static class ComputeCell<T> extends Cell<T> {
        Function<List<T>, T> function;
        List<Cell<T>> dependencies;
        List<Consumer<T>> callbacks = new ArrayList<>();

        public ComputeCell(Function<List<T>, T> function, List<Cell<T>> dependencies) {
            this.function = function;
            this.dependencies = dependencies;
            for (Cell<T> dep : dependencies) {
                dep.dependents.add(this);
            }
            this.value = function.apply(getDependencyValues());
        }

        private List<T> getDependencyValues() {
            List<T> values = new ArrayList<>();
            for (Cell<T> dep : dependencies) {
                values.add(dep.value);
            }
            return values;
        }

        void recompute() {
            T newVal = function.apply(getDependencyValues());
            if (!Objects.equals(newVal, this.value)) {
                this.value = newVal;
                this.changed = true;
            } else {
                this.changed = false;
            }
        }

        void triggerCallbacks() {
            for (Consumer<T> callback : callbacks) {
                callback.accept(this.value);
            }
        }

        public void addCallback(Consumer<T> callback) {
            callbacks.add(callback);
        }

        public void removeCallback(Consumer<T> callback) {
            callbacks.remove(callback);
        }
    }

    public static <T> InputCell<T> inputCell(T initialValue) {
        InputCell<T> cell = new InputCell<>();
        cell.value = initialValue;
        return cell;
    }

    public static <T> ComputeCell<T> computeCell(Function<List<T>, T> function, List<Cell<T>> cells) {
        return new ComputeCell<>(function, cells);
    }
}

#### # Introduction

#### The Zebra Puzzle is a famous logic puzzle in which there are five houses, each painted a different color.  
#### The houses have different inhabitants, who have different nationalities, own different pets, drink different beverages and enjoy different hobbies.  
####   
#### To help you solve the puzzle, you're given 15 statements describing the solution.  
#### However, only by combining the information in _all_ statements will you be able to find the solution to the puzzle.

#### ~~~~exercism/note  
#### The Zebra Puzzle is a [Constraint satisfaction problem (CSP)][constraint-satisfaction-problem].  
#### In such a problem, you have a set of possible values and a set of constraints that limit which values are valid.  
#### Another well-known CSP is Sudoku.  
####

The user wants to implement the Zebra Puzzle solver in C++.
The current implementation in `zebra_puzzle.cpp` just returns a hardcoded solution. While it happens to be correct for the standard puzzle, the instructions imply we should actually solve it programmatically, or at least provide a robust implementation. However, often in Exercism, a hardcoded solution is accepted if it's correct, but usually they expect a constraint solver or backtracking algorithm. Given the note "There are 24 billion possible solutions, so try ruling out as many solutions as possible.", it's better to implement a proper solver.

Let's implement a backtracking solver that assigns attributes to houses 1-5.
Attributes:
- Nationality: Englishman, Spaniard, Ukrainian, Norwegian, Japanese
- Color: Red, Ivory, Green, Yellow, Blue
- Drink: Coffee, Tea, Milk, Orange Juice, Water
- Pet: Dog, Snail, Fox, Horse, Zebra
- Hobby: Dancing, Painter, Reading, Football, Chess

Constraints:
1. 5 houses (implicit in our setup)
2. Englishman == Red
3. Spaniard == Dog
4. Green == Coffee
5. Ukrainian == Tea
6. Green is immediately right of Ivory (Green = Ivory + 1)
7. Snail == Dancing
8. Yellow == Painter
9. Middle house (3) == Milk
10. Norwegian == House 1
11. Reading next to Fox (|Reading - Fox| == 1)
12. Painter next to Horse (|Painter - Horse| == 1)
13. Football == Orange Juice
14. Japanese == Chess
15. Norwegian next to Blue (|Norwegian - Blue| == 1) -> Since Norwegian is 1, Blue must be 2.

If Green is 4, Ivory is 3.
If Green is 5, Ivory is 4.
So Ivory/Green are either (3,4) or (4,5).

Since the prompt says "Your task is to solve the Zebra Puzzle...", and the provided code already has the correct hardcoded answer, I could just leave it, but usually Exercism expects a solver. However, implementing a full backtracking solver in C++ for this exercise is standard. I will implement a clean backtracking solver that generates permutations and checks constraints, or uses the logical deduction I just did programmatically. Given the note about 24 billion possibilities, a simple permutation generator with pruning is expected.

I'll write a solver that uses `std::next_permutation` on arrays of attributes, with early pruning or constraint checking. To keep it simple and robust, I'll define arrays for each category and iterate through permutations, checking constraints. Since 5! = 120, 5!^5 is too big, but we can fix some positions or use nested loops with pruning.
Actually, a recursive backtracking approach assigning one attribute at a time to each house is efficient enough.

Let's implement a straightforward backtracking solver in `zebra_puzzle.cpp`.

Structure:
```cpp
#include "zebra_puzzle.h"
#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <algorithm>

namespace zebra_puzzle {

Solution solve() {
    // We will use indices 0-4 for houses 1-5.
    // Attributes:
    // Nationalities: Englishman, Spaniard, Ukrainian, Norwegian, Japanese
    // Colors: Red, Ivory, Green, Yellow, Blue
    // Drinks: Coffee, Tea, Milk, Orange Juice, Water
    // Pets: Dog, Snail, Fox, Horse, Zebra
    // Hobbies: Dancing, Painter, Reading, Football, Chess

    // We can represent each house as an index in permutations.
    // Or simpler: assign attributes to houses 0..4.
    // Let's use vectors of strings for each category, permuted.
    
    std::vector<std::string> nationalities = {"Englishman", "Spaniard", "Ukrainian", "Norwegian", "Japanese"};
    std::vector<std::string> colors = {"Red", "Ivory", "Green", "Yellow", "Blue"};
    std::vector<std::string> drinks = {"Coffee", "Tea", "Milk", "Orange Juice", "Water"};
    std::vector<std::string> pets = {"Dog", "Snail", "Fox", "Horse", "Zebra"};
    std::vector<std::string> hobbies = {"Dancing", "Painter", "Reading", "Football", "Chess"};

    // We'll iterate through permutations of colors, nationalities, etc.
    // To speed up, we can fix knowns:
    // Norwegian is house 0.
    // Milk is house 2.
    // Blue is house 1 (next to Norwegian).
    // But let's just do a standard backtracking or nested permutations with checks.
    // Given 5! = 120, 5 nested loops of 120 is 24 billion. Too slow.
    // We must prune heavily or use recursion.

    // Let's use a recursive function that assigns attributes house by house.
    // State: current house index (0-4)
    // Arrays to hold assignment: nat[5], col[5], drk[5], pet[5], hob[5]
    // Used flags for each attribute type to ensure uniqueness.
    
    // Actually, a simpler approach for this specific puzzle in C++:
    // Iterate permutations of nationalities, colors, drinks, pets, hobbies.
    // But prune early.
    // Or just hardcode the logical deduction steps into code? No, solver is better.
    
    // I'll implement a recursive backtracking solver.
    
    std::vector<std::string> nats = {"Englishman", "Spaniard", "Ukrainian", "Norwegian", "Japanese"};
    std::vector<std::string> cols = {"Red", "Ivory", "Green", "Yellow", "Blue"};
    std::vector<std::string> drks = {"Coffee", "Tea", "Milk", "Orange Juice", "Water"};
    std::vector<std::string> pets = {"Dog", "Snail", "Fox", "Horse", "Zebra"};
    std::vector<std::string> hobs = {"Dancing", "Painter", "Reading", "Football", "Chess"};

    std::vector<std::string> h_nat(5), h_col(5), h_drk(5), h_pet(5), h_hob(5);
    std::vector<bool> used_nat(5, false), used_col(5, false), used_drk(5, false), used_pet(5, false), used_hob(5, false);

    // Helper to find index of attribute in list
    auto idx = [](const std::vector<std::string>& v, const std::string& s) {
        for(size_t i=0; i<v.size(); ++i) if(v[i]==s) return i;
        return -1;
    };

    // We will assign house by house.
    // To prune effectively, we check constraints as soon as they can be verified.
    
    // Actually, let's just permute nationalities, colors, drinks, pets, hobbies separately
    // but with fixed positions for knowns to reduce search space drastically.
    // Norwegian -> 0
    // Milk -> 2
    // Blue -> 1 (next to Norwegian)
    // Green right of Ivory -> (Ivory, Green) is (2,3) or (3,4)
    // Yellow -> Painter
    // etc.
    
    // Given the small size, a simple recursive solver that assigns one attribute type at a time per house is fine.
    // Let's do a standard CSP backtracking.
    
    struct House {
        std::string nat, col, drk, pet, hob;
    };
    std::vector<House> houses(5);

    // Pre-fill knowns to prune
    // 10. Norwegian lives in first house.
    houses[0].nat = "Norwegian";
    // 9. Middle house drinks milk.
    houses[2].drk = "Milk";
    // 15. Norwegian next to blue -> Blue is house 1.
    houses[1].col = "Blue";

    // We'll use recursion to fill remaining attributes.
    // Order of filling: maybe colors, then nationalities, etc.
    // But to keep it simple and robust, I'll just iterate permutations of each category
    // and check constraints. Since we fixed 3 values, the search space is much smaller.
    // Actually, let's just write a clean backtracking function.
    
    // I'll stick to a straightforward implementation that permutes arrays and checks constraints.
    // It's concise and passes Exercism tests.
    
    // Let's define arrays for permutation
    std::vector<int> nat_p = {0,1,2,3,4}; // indices into nats
    std::vector<int> col_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> drk_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> pet_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> hob_p = {0,1,2,3,4};

    // Fix knowns in permutations? Or just check in loop.
    // Checking in loop is easier.
    
    // To avoid 24B iterations, we prune heavily inside loops.
    // We can iterate colors first.
    for (auto& cp : col_p) {
        // 6. Green right of Ivory
        int ivory_idx = -1, green_idx = -1;
        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
            if(cols[cp[i]] == "Ivory") ivory_idx = i;
            if(cols[cp[i]] == "Green") green_idx = i;
        }
        if(green_idx != ivory_idx + 1) continue;
        
        // 15. Norwegian next to Blue. Norwegian is 0. So Blue must be 1.
        if(cols[cp[1]] != "Blue") continue;
        
        // 2. Englishman in Red
        // 8. Yellow is Painter
        // We'll handle these in nationality/hobby loops.
        
        for (auto& np : nat_p) {
            // 10. Norwegian in first house
            if(nats[np[0]] != "Norwegian") continue;
            
            // 2. Englishman in Red
            int eng_idx = -1, red_idx = -1;
            for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                if(nats[np[i]] == "Englishman") eng_idx = i;
                if(cols[cp[i]] == "Red") red_idx = i;
            }
            if(eng_idx != red_idx) continue;
            
            for (auto& dp : drk_p) {
                // 9. Middle drinks Milk
                if(drks[dp[2]] != "Milk") continue;
                
                // 4. Green drinks Coffee
                if(drks[dp[green_idx]] != "Coffee") continue;
                
                // 5. Ukrainian drinks Tea
                int ukr_idx = -1, tea_idx = -1;
                for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                    if(nats[np[i]] == "Ukrainian") ukr_idx = i;
                    if(drks[dp[i]] == "Tea") tea_idx = i;
                }
                if(ukr_idx != tea_idx) continue;
                
                for (auto& pp : pet_p) {
                    // 3. Spaniard owns Dog
                    int spa_idx = -1, dog_idx = -1;
                    for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                        if(nats[np[i]] == "Spaniard") spa_idx = i;
                        if(pets[pp[i]] == "Dog") dog_idx = i;
                    }
                    if(spa_idx != dog_idx) continue;
                    
                    for (auto& hp : hob_p) {
                        // 8. Yellow is Painter
                        int yel_idx = -1, pai_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(cols[cp[i]] == "Yellow") yel_idx = i;
                            if(hobs[hp[i]] == "Painter") pai_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(yel_idx != pai_idx) continue;
                        
                        // 7. Snail owner likes Dancing
                        int sna_idx = -1, dan_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(pets[pp[i]] == "Snail") sna_idx = i;
                            if(hobs[hp[i]] == "Dancing") dan_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(sna_idx != dan_idx) continue;
                        
                        // 13. Football drinks OJ
                        int foo_idx = -1, oj_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(hobs[hp[i]] == "Football") foo_idx = i;
                            if(drks[dp[i]] == "Orange Juice") oj_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(foo_idx != oj_idx) continue;
                        
                        // 14. Japanese plays Chess
                        int jap_idx = -1, che_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(nats[np[i]] == "Japanese") jap_idx = i;
                            if(hobs[hp[i]] == "Chess") che_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(jap_idx != che_idx) continue;
                        
                        // 11. Reading next to Fox
                        int rea_idx = -1, fox_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(hobs[hp[i]] == "Reading") rea_idx = i;
                            if(pets[pp[i]] == "Fox") fox_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(std::abs(rea_idx - fox_idx) != 1) continue;
                        
                        // 12. Painter next to Horse
                        int hor_idx = -1;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(pets[pp[i]] == "Horse") hor_idx = i;
                        }
                        if(std::abs(pai_idx - hor_idx) != 1) continue;
                        
                        // All constraints satisfied!
                        // Find who drinks Water
                        std::string water_drinker;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(drks[dp[i]] == "Water") {
                                water_drinker = nats[np[i]];
                                break;
                            }
                        }
                        // Find who owns Zebra
                        std::string zebra_owner;
                        for(int i=0; i<5; ++i) {
                            if(pets[pp[i]] == "Zebra") {
                                zebra_owner = nats[np[i]];
                                break;
                            }
                        }
                        return Solution{water_drinker, zebra_owner};
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return Solution{"", ""}; // Should not reach here
}

}  // namespace zebra_puzzle
```

Wait, `std::next_permutation` requires sorted initial array to cover all permutations. I initialized them as `{0,1,2,3,4}` which is sorted. But I'm using range-based for loops `for (auto& cp : col_p)`. That won't generate permutations! I need to use `do { ... } while (std::next_permutation(...));`.

Let's fix the loop structure:
```cpp
    std::vector<int> nat_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> col_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> drk_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> pet_p = {0,1,2,3,4};
    std::vector<int> hob_p = {0,1,2,3,4};

    do {
        // ... checks ...
        do {
            // ... checks ...
            do {
                // ...
                do {
                    // ...
                    do {
                        // ...
                    } while (std::next_permutation(hob_p.begin(), hob_p.end()));
                } while (std::next_permutation(pet_p.begin(), pet_p.end()));
            } while (std::next_permutation(drk_p.begin(), drk_p.end()));
        } while (std::next_permutation(nat_p.begin(), nat_p.end()));
    } while (std::next_permutation(col_p.begin(), col_p.end()));
```
This will work. But it's 5 nested loops of 120 iterations = 24B. Even with pruning, it might be slow if pruning isn't early enough. However, with the constraints checked early, it will prune massively.
Actually, checking `cols[cp[1]] != "Blue"` inside the outer loop prunes 4/5 of color permutations immediately.
`green_idx != ivory_idx + 1` prunes more.
So it will be very fast.

Let's refine the code to be clean and correct. I'll put the solver logic in `zebra_puzzle.cpp`.

```cpp
#include "zebra_puzzle.h"
#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstdlib>

namespace zebra_puzzle {

Solution solve() {
    std::vector<std::string> nats = {"Englishman", "Spaniard", "Ukrainian", "Norwegian", "Japanese"};
    std::vector<std::string> cols = {"Red", "Ivory", "Green", "Yellow", "Blue"};
    std::vector<std::string> drks = {"Coffee", "Tea", "Milk", "Orange Juice", "Water"};
    std::vector<std::string> pets = {"Dog", "Snail", "Fox", "Horse", "Zebra"};
    std::vector<std::string> hobs = {"Dancing", "Painter", "Reading", "Football", "Chess"};

    std::vector<int> nat_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> col_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> drk_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> pet_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> hob_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};

    do {
        // Constraint 6: Green immediately right of Ivory
        int ivory_idx = -1, green_idx = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Ivory") ivory_idx = i;
            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Green") green_idx = i;
        }
        if (green_idx != ivory_idx + 1) continue;

        // Constraint 15: Norwegian next to Blue. Norwegian is fixed at 0 by later check, but we know Blue must be 1.
        if (cols[col_p[1]] != "Blue") continue;

        do {
            // Constraint 10: Norwegian in first house
            if (nats[nat_p[0]] != "Norwegian") continue;

            // Constraint 2: Englishman in Red house
            int eng_idx = -1, red_idx = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Englishman") eng_idx = i;
                if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Red") red_idx = i;
            }
            if (eng_idx != red_idx) continue;

            do {
                // Constraint 9: Middle house drinks Milk
                if (drks[drk_p[2]] != "Milk") continue;

                // Constraint 4: Green house drinks Coffee
                if (drks[drk_p[green_idx]] != "Coffee") continue;

                // Constraint 5: Ukrainian drinks Tea
                int ukr_idx = -1, tea_idx = -1;
                for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                    if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Ukrainian") ukr_idx = i;
                    if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Tea") tea_idx = i;
                }
                if (ukr_idx != tea_idx) continue;

                do {
                    // Constraint 3: Spaniard owns Dog
                    int spa_idx = -1, dog_idx = -1;
                    for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                        if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Spaniard") spa_idx = i;
                        if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Dog") dog_idx = i;
                    }
                    if (spa_idx != dog_idx) continue;

                    do {
                        // Constraint 8: Yellow house is Painter
                        int yel_idx = -1, pai_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Yellow") yel_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Painter") pai_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (yel_idx != pai_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 7: Snail owner likes Dancing
                        int sna_idx = -1, dan_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Snail") sna_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Dancing") dan_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (sna_idx != dan_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 13: Football drinks Orange Juice
                        int foo_idx = -1, oj_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Football") foo_idx = i;
                            if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Orange Juice") oj_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (foo_idx != oj_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 14: Japanese plays Chess
                        int jap_idx = -1, che_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Japanese") jap_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Chess") che_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (jap_idx != che_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 11: Reading next to Fox
                        int rea_idx = -1, fox_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Reading") rea_idx = i;
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Fox") fox_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (std::abs(rea_idx - fox_idx) != 1) continue;

                        // Constraint 12: Painter next to Horse
                        int hor_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Horse") hor_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (std::abs(pai_idx - hor_idx) != 1) continue;

                        // All constraints satisfied
                        std::string water_drinker, zebra_owner;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Water") water_drinker = nats[nat_p[i]];
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Zebra") zebra_owner = nats[nat_p[i]];
                        }
                        return Solution{water_drinker, zebra_owner};

                    } while (std::next_permutation(hob_p.begin(), hob_p.end()));
                } while (std::next_permutation(pet_p.begin(), pet_p.end()));
            } while (std::next_permutation(drk_p.begin(), drk_p.end()));
        } while (std::next_permutation(nat_p.begin(), nat_p.end()));
    } while (std::next_permutation(col_p.begin(), col_p.end()));

    return Solution{"", ""};
}

}  // namespace zebra_puzzle

Wait, `std::abs` is in `<cmath>` or `<cstdlib>`. I'll include `<cmath>`.
Also, I should reset permutations after each `next_permutation` loop? No, `do...while` handles it. But when we `continue` from an outer loop, the inner permutations are left in some state. That's fine because we only care about finding the first valid solution, and we will find it quickly. Actually, to be strictly correct with `next_permutation`, we should ensure they start sorted, but since we only need to find one solution and we `return` immediately, it's fine. The loops will terminate correctly.

One detail: `std::next_permutation` returns false when it wraps around to the first permutation. The `do...while` structure is correct.

Since Norwegian is fixed at index 0, Blue must be at index 1. My code checks `cols[col_p[1]] != "Blue"`. This is correct.

Constraint 6: "The green house is immediately to the right of the ivory house."
`green_idx != ivory_idx + 1` correctly checks this.

```cpp
#include "zebra_puzzle.h"
#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstdlib>

namespace zebra_puzzle {

Solution solve() {
    std::vector<std::string> nats = {"Englishman", "Spaniard", "Ukrainian", "Norwegian", "Japanese"};
    std::vector<std::string> cols = {"Red", "Ivory", "Green", "Yellow", "Blue"};
    std::vector<std::string> drks = {"Coffee", "Tea", "Milk", "Orange Juice", "Water"};
    std::vector<std::string> pets = {"Dog", "Snail", "Fox", "Horse", "Zebra"};
    std::vector<std::string> hobs = {"Dancing", "Painter", "Reading", "Football", "Chess"};

    std::vector<int> nat_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> col_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> drk_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> pet_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};
    std::vector<int> hob_p = {0, 1, 2, 3, 4};

    do {
        // Constraint 6: Green immediately right of Ivory
        int ivory_idx = -1, green_idx = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Ivory") ivory_idx = i;
            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Green") green_idx = i;
        }
        if (green_idx != ivory_idx + 1) continue;
        
        // Constraint 15: Norwegian next to Blue. Since Norwegian is fixed at 0, Blue must be 1.
        if (cols[col_p[1]] != "Blue") continue;

        do {
            // Constraint 10: Norwegian in first house
            if (nats[nat_p[0]] != "Norwegian") continue;

            // Constraint 2: Englishman in Red house
            int eng_idx = -1, red_idx = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Englishman") eng_idx = i;
                if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Red") red_idx = i;
            }
            if (eng_idx != red_idx) continue;

            do {
                // Constraint 9: Middle house drinks Milk
                if (drks[drk_p[2]] != "Milk") continue;
                
                // Constraint 4: Green house drinks Coffee
                if (drks[drk_p[green_idx]] != "Coffee") continue;

                // Constraint 5: Ukrainian drinks Tea
                int ukr_idx = -1, tea_idx = -1;
                for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                    if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Ukrainian") ukr_idx = i;
                    if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Tea") tea_idx = i;
                }
                if (ukr_idx != tea_idx) continue;

                do {
                    // Constraint 3: Spaniard owns Dog
                    int spa_idx = -1, dog_idx = -1;
                    for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                        if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Spaniard") spa_idx = i;
                        if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Dog") dog_idx = i;
                    }
                    if (spa_idx != dog_idx) continue;

                    do {
                        // Constraint 8: Yellow house is Painter
                        int yel_idx = -1, pai_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (cols[col_p[i]] == "Yellow") yel_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Painter") pai_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (yel_idx != pai_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 7: Snail owner likes Dancing
                        int sna_idx = -1, dan_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Snail") sna_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Dancing") dan_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (sna_idx != dan_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 13: Football drinks Orange Juice
                        int foo_idx = -1, oj_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Football") foo_idx = i;
                            if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Orange Juice") oj_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (foo_idx != oj_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 14: Japanese plays Chess
                        int jap_idx = -1, che_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (nats[nat_p[i]] == "Japanese") jap_idx = i;
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Chess") che_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (jap_idx != che_idx) continue;

                        // Constraint 11: Reading next to Fox
                        int rea_idx = -1, fox_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (hobs[hob_p[i]] == "Reading") rea_idx = i;
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Fox") fox_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (std::abs(rea_idx - fox_idx) != 1) continue;

                        // Constraint 12: Painter next to Horse
                        int hor_idx = -1;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Horse") hor_idx = i;
                        }
                        if (std::abs(pai_idx - hor_idx) != 1) continue;

                        // All constraints satisfied
                        std::string water_drinker, zebra_owner;
                        for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {
                            if (drks[drk_p[i]] == "Water") water_drinker = nats[nat_p[i]];
                            if (pets[pet_p[i]] == "Zebra") zebra_owner = nats[nat_p[i]];
                        }
                        return Solution{water_drinker, zebra_owner};

                    } while (std::next_permutation(hob_p.begin(), hob_p.end()));
                } while (std::next_permutation(pet_p.begin(), pet_p.end()));
            } while (std::next_permutation(drk_p.begin(), drk_p.end()));
        } while (std::next_permutation(nat_p.begin(), nat_p.end()));
    } while (std::next_permutation(col_p.begin(), col_p.end()));

    return Solution{"", ""};
}

}  // namespace zebra_puzzle

#### # Instructions  
####   
#### Implement a basic reactive system.  
####   
#### Reactive programming is a programming paradigm that focuses on how values are computed in terms of each other to allow a change to one value to automatically propagate to other values, like in a spreadsheet.  
####   
#### Implement a basic reactive system with cells with settable values ("input" cells) and cells with values computed in terms of other cells ("compute" cells).

Actually, a more robust way to handle reactive updates in a DAG is topological sort or ensuring we only compute each cell once per propagation pass. ```go
func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	for i, cb := range c.cell.callbacks {
		if cb == c.callback {
			c.cell.callbacks = append(c.cell.callbacks[:i], c.cell.callbacks[i+1:]...)
			break
		}
	}
}
```

Let's consider the test cases for this exercise (usually from Exercism).
Common pitfalls:
- Callbacks should be called with the new value.
- Callbacks should only be called once per `SetValue`, even if multiple inputs change.
- If a compute cell's value doesn't change, callbacks should not be called.
- Canceling a callback should prevent it from being called.
- Removing a callback shouldn't affect others.
- Multiple callbacks on the same cell should all be called.
- Callbacks should be called in the order they were added.
- `SetValue` with the same value should not trigger propagation or callbacks. (Handled by `if value == c.value { return }`)

One thing: `propagate` currently updates `dep.value` immediately. This means if a cell has multiple dependencies, and both change, the cell might be computed multiple times. This is inefficient but correct. However, there's a known issue with this approach in some reactive systems: if the graph has cycles, it loops. But the problem implies a DAG (like a spreadsheet).

Let's look at `propagate` again:
```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	var changed []*cell
	queue := []*cell{c}
	seen := make(map[*cell]bool)

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				dep.value = newVal
				if !seen[dep] {
					seen[dep] = true
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					queue = append(queue, dep)
				}
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
			cb(cell.value)
		}
	}
}
```
Wait, `changed` collects cells that changed. But what if a cell's value changes, we add it to `changed`, then later in the BFS it changes again? It's already in `changed`, so it will be called once. That's correct.
But there's a subtle bug: `seen` prevents adding to queue multiple times. If `dep` is already in `queue` (because it was seen), and its value changes again, we update `dep.value` but don't add to queue again. That's fine, it will be processed when popped from queue. But wait, when it's popped from queue, it will compute *its* dependents based on the *current* `dep.value`. Since `dep.value` might have been updated multiple times before being popped, the final value used to compute dependents will be the latest one. That's actually correct for BFS if we process level by level, but here we update `dep.value` eagerly. So when `dep` is eventually popped, `dep.value` is already the final computed value for this propagation pass. So its dependents will be computed correctly.

However, there's a known issue with this eager update approach: if a dependent's value changes, we update it immediately. But if another path to the same dependent computes a different intermediate value, we might update it again. The final value will be correct because all dependencies are updated before the dependent is processed from the queue? Not necessarily. BFS processes level by level. If `dep` depends on `A` and `B`, and both `A` and `B` change, `dep` will be encountered twice. First time, `A` changed, `dep` computes using new `A` and old `B`. `dep.value` updates. Added to queue. Second time, `B` changed, `dep` computes using new `A` and new `B`. `dep.value` updates again. Still in queue. When popped, `dep` computes its dependents using new `A` and new `B`. This works.

But wait, what if `dep` depends on `A` and `B`, but `B` depends on `A`? Then `A` changes -> `B` updates -> `dep` updates (using new `A`, new `B`). `A` -> `dep` updates (using new `A`, old `B`? No, `B` already updated because `B` is a dependent of `A`, so `B` updates before `dep` is processed? Actually, `B` is added to queue. `dep` is added to queue when processing `A`'s dependents. So queue order: `B`, `dep`. When `B` is processed, `dep` updates again. So `dep` value is correct when popped. This is fine.

One major issue in the current code: `canceler.Cancel()` removes the callback from the slice. But if `Cancel()` is called while `propagate()` is iterating over `cell.callbacks`, it could cause issues? Not in single-threaded tests. But it's safer to just mark callbacks as cancelled or use a separate list. I'll keep it simple for now, as Exercism tests are usually single-threaded.

Actually, there's a known bug in similar implementations: if a cell's value changes, we add it to `changed`. But if it changes again later in the same propagation, it's already in `changed`, so it's fine. But what if a cell's value changes, then changes back to the original value? The first change adds it to `changed`. The second change (back to original) sees `newVal == dep.value` (original), so it doesn't add to `changed` again. But it's already in `changed`! So callbacks will fire with the original value, which is wrong. Callbacks should only fire if the final value differs from the initial value.
Ah! This is a critical bug. We need to compare the final value with the value *before* propagation started.
Let's fix this. We should record the initial value of each cell before propagation, or only add to `changed` if the value differs from the value at the start of `SetValue`.
Actually, a simpler way: collect all cells that need updating, compute their new values, and only call callbacks if the new value differs from the old value.
Better approach:
```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	var changed []*cell
	queue := []*cell{c}
	seen := make(map[*cell]bool)
	// Store initial values to compare later
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)
	initialValues[c] = c.value

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			if seen[dep] {
				continue
			}
			seen[dep] = true
			queue = append(queue, dep)
			
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if dep.value == initialValues[dep] {
					// It changed from initial, but might change again.
					// We'll track it.
				}
				dep.value = newVal
			}
		}
	}
}
```

```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	seen := make(map[*cell]bool)
	seen[c] = true
	
	// Record initial values for all cells that might change
	// Actually, we only need to record initial values for cells in the propagation chain.
	// But it's easier to just check at the end.
	
	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				dep.value = newVal
				if !seen[dep] {
					seen[dep] = true
					queue = append(queue, dep)
				}
			}
		}

Actually, we can just keep a list of cells that changed, but deduplicate and verify at the end.
Simpler: `changed` map `*cell -> int` (initial value). When a cell's value changes, if it's not in `changed`, record its initial value. At the end, iterate `changed`, and if `cell.value != changed[cell]`, fire callbacks.
Let's implement this:
```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	seen := make(map[*cell]bool)
	seen[c] = true
	changed := make(map[*cell]int) // cell -> initial value

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if _, ok := changed[dep]; !ok {
					changed[dep] = dep.value // store value before this change
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				if !seen[dep] {
					seen[dep] = true
					queue = append(queue, dep)
				}
			}
		}
	}

	for cell, initialValue := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValue {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				cb(cell.value)
			}
		}
	}
}
```
This handles the "change back" case correctly. If a cell changes twice, `changed[dep]` stores the first old value. At the end, we compare final value with that stored initial value. Wait, if it changes A->B, then B->A, `changed[dep]` = A. Final value = A. `cell.value != initialValue` is false. Callbacks not fired. Correct.
If it changes A->B, then B->C, `changed[dep]` = A. Final value = C. `C != A` is true. Callbacks fired with C. Correct.
But what if it changes A->B, then we don't add to `changed` again. Correct.
One edge case: `dep` might be added to `changed` when it changes from A to B. Later it changes from B to C. `changed[dep]` is still A. Final value C. `C != A` -> fire. Correct.

func (c *cell) propagate() {

```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	seen := make(map[*cell]bool)
	seen[c] = true
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)

		queue = queue[1:]

			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value // store value before first change
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				if !seen[dep] {
					seen[dep] = true

				}
			}
		}
	}

		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				cb(cell.value)
			}
		}
	}
}
```

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, callback: callback}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	for i, cb := range c.cell.callbacks {
		if cb == c.callback {
			c.cell.callbacks = append(c.cell.callbacks[:i], c.cell.callbacks[i+1:]...)
			break
		}
	}
}

	queue := []*cell{c}

BFS will process A (level 0), then C and B (level 1). If B is processed before C, B computes with old C. Then C processes, updates C. But B is already processed! `seen` prevents re-processing. So B's dependents get wrong values.

```go
func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)
	// We can track how many times we've seen a cell to avoid infinite loops, 
	// or just trust it's a DAG. I'll trust it's a DAG for simplicity, 
	// but add a safety mechanism if needed. Actually, just removing 'seen' is fine for DAG.
	// But to be safe, I'll use a map to count visits or just rely on value changes.
	// Let's just use a simple worklist algorithm:
	
	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}
	// fire callbacks
}
```

Let's rewrite `react.go` with these improvements.

```go
package react

// Define interfaces
type Reactor interface {
	CreateInput(initial int) InputCell
	CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell
	CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell
}

type Cell interface {
	Value() int
	AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler
}

type InputCell interface {
	Cell
	SetValue(value int)
}

type ComputeCell interface {
	Cell
}

type Canceler interface {
	Cancel()
}

type reactor struct {
	cells []*cell
}

type cell struct {
	value        int
	reactor      *reactor
	isInput      bool
	compute1     func(int) int
	compute2     func(int, int) int
	dep1         *cell
	dep2         *cell
	dependents   []*cell
	callbacks    []func(int)
}

type canceler struct {
	cell     *cell
	callback func(int)
}

func New() Reactor {
	return &reactor{}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateInput(initial int) InputCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:   initial,
		reactor: r,
		isInput: true,
	}
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep.Value()),
		reactor:  r,
		dep1:     dep.(*cell),
		compute1: compute,
	}
	dep.(*cell).dependents = append(dep.(*cell).dependents, c)
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep1.Value(), dep2.Value()),
		reactor:  r,
		dep1:     dep1.(*cell),
		dep2:     dep2.(*cell),
		compute2: compute,
	}
	dep1.(*cell).dependents = append(dep1.(*cell).dependents, c)
	dep2.(*cell).dependents = append(dep2.(*cell).dependents, c)
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (c *cell) Value() int {
	return c.value
}

func (c *cell) SetValue(value int) {
	if value == c.value {
		return
	}
	c.value = value
	c.propagate()
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, callback: callback}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	for i, cb := range c.cell.callbacks {
		if cb == c.callback {
			c.cell.callbacks = append(c.cell.callbacks[:i], c.cell.callbacks[i+1:]...)
			break
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				cb(cell.value)
			}
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) computeValue() int {
	if c.compute1 != nil {
		return c.compute1(c.dep1.value)
	}
	if c.compute2 != nil {
		return c.compute2(c.dep1.value, c.dep2.value)
	}
	return c.value
}
```

Wait, `SetValue` has `if value == c.value { return }`. This is good.
One minor point: `propagate` starts with `queue := []*cell{c}`. `c` is the input cell that changed. `c.dependents` will be processed. This is correct.
But what if `c` is a compute cell? `SetValue` is only on `InputCell`, so `c` is always input. `c.computeValue()` isn't called for `c` in the loop because we iterate `curr.dependents`. So it's fine.

Let's double check `Canceler` implementation. Comparing functions with `==` is valid in Go. It's fine.
Is there any issue with `changedMap` and `initialValues`? They store the value *before* the first change. If a cell changes multiple times, `initialValues` keeps the original. At the end, we compare final value with original. If different, callbacks fire. This matches requirements: "Call a cell’s callbacks when the cell’s value in a new stable state has changed from the previous stable state."

```go
package react

// Define interfaces
type Reactor interface {
	CreateInput(initial int) InputCell
	CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell
	CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell
}

type Cell interface {
	Value() int
	AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler
}

type InputCell interface {
	Cell
	SetValue(value int)
}

type ComputeCell interface {
	Cell
}

type Canceler interface {
	Cancel()
}

type reactor struct {
	cells []*cell
}

type cell struct {
	value        int
	reactor      *reactor
	isInput      bool
	compute1     func(int) int
	compute2     func(int, int) int
	dep1         *cell
	dep2         *cell
	dependents   []*cell
	callbacks    []func(int)
}

type canceler struct {
	cell     *cell
	callback func(int)
}

func New() Reactor {
	return &reactor{}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateInput(initial int) InputCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:   initial,
		reactor: r,
		isInput: true,
	}
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep.Value()),
		reactor:  r,
		dep1:     dep.(*cell),
		compute1: compute,
	}
	dep.(*cell).dependents = append(dep.(*cell).dependents, c)
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep1.Value(), dep2.Value()),
		reactor:  r,
		dep1:     dep1.(*cell),
		dep2:     dep2.(*cell),
		compute2: compute,
	}
	dep1.(*cell).dependents = append(dep1.(*cell).dependents, c)
	dep2.(*cell).dependents = append(dep2.(*cell).dependents, c)
	r.cells = append(r.cells, c)
	return c
}

func (c *cell) Value() int {
	return c.value
}

func (c *cell) SetValue(value int) {
	if value == c.value {
		return
	}
	c.value = value
	c.propagate()
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, callback: callback}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	for i, cb := range c.cell.callbacks {
		if cb == c.callback {
			c.cell.callbacks = append(c.cell.callbacks[:i], c.cell.callbacks[i+1:]...)
			break
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				cb(cell.value)
			}
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) computeValue() int {
	if c.compute1 != nil {
		return c.compute1(c.dep1.value)
	}
	if c.compute2 != nil {
		return c.compute2(c.dep1.value, c.dep2.value)
	}
	return c.value
}
```

One edge case: what if `SetValue` is called with the same value? Handled.
What if a compute cell's value changes but callbacks should not be called? Handled by `cell.value != initialValues[cell]`.
What if callbacks are added during propagation? Not expected.
The code looks solid.

type cell struct {
    // ...
    callbacks []func(int)
}

type canceler struct {
    cell *cell
    idx  int
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
    idx := len(c.callbacks)
    c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
    return &canceler{cell: c, idx: idx}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
    c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil
}
```

```go
package react

type reactor struct {
	// cells not strictly needed but kept if used, actually we don't need to store all cells in reactor
}

type cell struct {
	value      int
	isInput    bool
	compute1   func(int) int
	compute2   func(int, int) int
	dep1       *cell
	dep2       *cell
	dependents []*cell
	callbacks  []func(int)
}

type canceler struct {
	cell *cell
	idx  int
}

func New() Reactor {
	return &reactor{}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateInput(initial int) InputCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:   initial,
		isInput: true,
	}
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep.Value()),
		dep1:     dep.(*cell),
		compute1: compute,
	}
	dep.(*cell).dependents = append(dep.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep1.Value(), dep2.Value()),
		dep1:     dep1.(*cell),
		dep2:     dep2.(*cell),
		compute2: compute,
	}
	dep1.(*cell).dependents = append(dep1.(*cell).dependents, c)
	dep2.(*cell).dependents = append(dep2.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (c *cell) Value() int {
	return c.value
}

func (c *cell) SetValue(value int) {
	if value == c.value {
		return
	}
	c.value = value
	c.propagate()
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	idx := len(c.callbacks)
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, idx: idx}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil
}

func (c *cell) propagate() {
	// We need to propagate changes. The current implementation uses a queue.
	// It tracks changed cells and their initial values to fire callbacks only once per stable state change.
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				if cb != nil {
					cb(cell.value)
				}
			}
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) computeValue() int {
	if c.compute1 != nil {
		return c.compute1(c.dep1.value)
	}
	if c.compute2 != nil {
		return c.compute2(c.dep1.value, c.dep2.value)
	}
	return c.value
}
```

Wait, `propagate` logic:
The current logic adds `c` (the input cell that changed) to the queue. It iterates through dependents.
But what if a dependent doesn't change value? It won't be added to the queue, so its dependents won't be recomputed. That's correct for reactive systems: if a compute cell's value doesn't change, we don't need to propagate further, and we definitely don't fire callbacks.
However, there's a subtle issue: `queue` might process the same cell multiple times if it's added multiple times before being processed? The `changedMap` prevents adding to `changed` multiple times, but `queue` might have duplicates. That's fine, `computeValue` will just return the same value, and `newVal != dep.value` will be false, so it won't be added again.
Wait, `changedMap` only prevents adding to `changed` list. It doesn't prevent adding to `queue`. But if `newVal == dep.value`, it won't be added to `queue`. So duplicates in `queue` only happen if `newVal != dep.value` multiple times before processing. That's fine.

One potential issue: `initialValues[dep] = dep.value` is set when first detected as changed. But `dep.value` is updated immediately after: `dep.value = newVal`. So `initialValues` correctly stores the old value.
When firing callbacks, we check `if cell.value != initialValues[cell]`. This ensures we only fire if the value actually changed from the start of the propagation. This matches the requirement: "Call a cell’s callbacks when the cell’s value in a new stable state has changed from the previous stable state."

Let's double check the `propagate` logic with a diamond dependency:
A -> B, A -> C, B -> D, C -> D.
If A changes, queue = [A].
Process A: updates B, C. Both change. queue = [B, C]. changed = [B, C].
Process B: updates D. D changes. queue = [C, D]. changed = [B, C, D].
Process C: updates D. D might change again or stay same. If it changes, `newVal != dep.value` is true. `changedMap[D]` is true, so not added to `changed`. `dep.value` updated. `queue` appends D again? `queue = [D, D]`.
Process D: computes value. Dependents of D processed.
Process D again: computes value. Same.
This works. The final value of D is correct. Callbacks for D are fired once because `changedMap` ensures it's in `changed` only once. `initialValues[D]` holds the value before any changes in this propagation. `cell.value != initialValues[cell]` will be true if D changed at least once. This matches requirements.

What about `Cancel()` implementation?
Using index and setting to `nil` is standard and safe.
One thing: `c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil` might panic if `c.idx >= len(c.cell.callbacks)`, but that shouldn't happen.
Is there any case where `callbacks` slice reallocates and indices become invalid? No, we store the index at creation time, and we never remove elements, just set to `nil`. So indices remain valid.

```go

type reactor struct{}

type cell struct {
	value      int
	isInput    bool
	compute1   func(int) int
	compute2   func(int, int) int
	dep1       *cell
	dep2       *cell
	dependents []*cell
	callbacks  []func(int)

	cell *cell
	idx  int

	return &reactor{}
}

	return &cell{
		value:   initial,
		isInput: true,
	}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep.Value()),
		dep1:     dep.(*cell),
		compute1: compute,
	}
	dep.(*cell).dependents = append(dep.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep1.Value(), dep2.Value()),
		dep1:     dep1.(*cell),
		dep2:     dep2.(*cell),
		compute2: compute,
	}
	dep1.(*cell).dependents = append(dep1.(*cell).dependents, c)
	dep2.(*cell).dependents = append(dep2.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (c *cell) Value() int {
	return c.value
}

func (c *cell) SetValue(value int) {
	if value == c.value {
		return
	}
	c.value = value
	c.propagate()
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	idx := len(c.callbacks)
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, idx: idx}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil
}

func (c *cell) propagate() {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				if cb != nil {
					cb(cell.value)
				}
			}
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) computeValue() int {
	if c.compute1 != nil {
		return c.compute1(c.dep1.value)
	}
	if c.compute2 != nil {
		return c.compute2(c.dep1.value, c.dep2.value)
	}
	return c.value
}
```

```go
package react

type reactor struct{}

type cell struct {
	value      int
	isInput    bool
	compute1   func(int) int
	compute2   func(int, int) int
	dep1       *cell
	dep2       *cell
	dependents []*cell
	callbacks  []func(int)
}

type canceler struct {
	cell *cell
	idx  int
}

func New() Reactor {
	return &reactor{}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateInput(initial int) InputCell {
	return &cell{
		value:   initial,
		isInput: true,
	}
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute1(dep Cell, compute func(int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep.Value()),
		dep1:     dep.(*cell),
		compute1: compute,
	}
	dep.(*cell).dependents = append(dep.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (r *reactor) CreateCompute2(dep1, dep2 Cell, compute func(int, int) int) ComputeCell {
	c := &cell{
		value:    compute(dep1.Value(), dep2.Value()),
		dep1:     dep1.(*cell),
		dep2:     dep2.(*cell),
		compute2: compute,
	}
	dep1.(*cell).dependents = append(dep1.(*cell).dependents, c)
	dep2.(*cell).dependents = append(dep2.(*cell).dependents, c)
	return c
}

func (c *cell) Value() int {
	return c.value
}

func (c *cell) SetValue(value int) {
	if value == c.value {
		return
	}
	oldVal := c.value
	c.value = value
	c.propagate(oldVal)
}

func (c *cell) AddCallback(callback func(int)) Canceler {
	idx := len(c.callbacks)
	c.callbacks = append(c.callbacks, callback)
	return &canceler{cell: c, idx: idx}
}

func (c *canceler) Cancel() {
	c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil
}

func (c *cell) propagate(oldVal int) {
	queue := []*cell{c}
	var changed []*cell
	changedMap := make(map[*cell]bool)
	initialValues := make(map[*cell]int)

	changed = append(changed, c)
	changedMap[c] = true
	initialValues[c] = oldVal

	for len(queue) > 0 {
		curr := queue[0]
		queue = queue[1:]

		for _, dep := range curr.dependents {
			newVal := dep.computeValue()
			if newVal != dep.value {
				if !changedMap[dep] {
					changed = append(changed, dep)
					changedMap[dep] = true
					initialValues[dep] = dep.value
				}
				dep.value = newVal
				queue = append(queue, dep)
			}
		}
	}

	for _, cell := range changed {
		if cell.value != initialValues[cell] {
			for _, cb := range cell.callbacks {
				if cb != nil {
					cb(cell.value)
				}
			}
		}
	}
}

func (c *cell) computeValue() int {
	if c.compute1 != nil {
		return c.compute1(c.dep1.value)
	}
	if c.compute2 != nil {
		return c.compute2(c.dep1.value, c.dep2.value)
	}
	return c.value
}
```

Wait, `propagate` is called on `c`. `queue` starts with `c`. `curr` becomes `c`. `curr.dependents` are processed.

Let's double check the `Cancel` implementation. `c.cell.callbacks[c.idx] = nil`. This is safe. When iterating `cell.callbacks`, we check `if cb != nil`. This avoids calling cancelled callbacks.

One minor point: `reactor` struct in original had `cells []*cell`. I removed it. That's fine.
